In 2023, the Federal working group on psycho-social\ncentres for refugees and victims of torture in cooperation with several NGOs acknowledged that there\nhave been attempts mainly by the Federal States to address these shortcomings. However, the working\ngroup repeated the criticism of no systematic approach.536 Along with these policy demands, the\nassociations introduced a toolbox on vulnerabilities which provides guidance for counselling and the\nidentification of vulnerabilities.537\n\nThe procedures and practice of identification in reception centres, which are run by the Federal States,\nvary. Upon initial registration, all asylum seekers should undergo a medical examination, which usually\ntakes place shortly after the registration of the asylum application in the arrival centre. However, this\nexamination is focused on the detection of communicable diseases and does not include a screening for\npotential vulnerabilities. Sometimes medical personnel or other staff members working in the reception\ncentres inform the BAMF if they recognise symptoms of trauma, the BAMF provides notification sheets\nwith which vulnerabilities can be communicated but there is no systematic procedure in place ensuring\nthat such information is passed on.538\n\nAs of 2020, only three Federal States (Berlin, Brandenburg and Lower Saxony) had a structured\nprocedure in place to identify particularly vulnerable asylum seekers.539 A number of States conduct\nscreenings, offer psychiatric or psychological consultations or refer to the general care infrastructure, and\nsome Federal States have integrated identification in their concepts for protection from violence in\nreception centres (Hamburg, Hesse, North Rhine Westphalia, Rhineland-Palatinate, Saarland,\nSaxony, Schleswig-Holstein).540 Since 2021, a project led by the Federal working group on psycho-\nsocial centres for refugees and victims of torture in cooperation with several NGOs as well as the BAMF\nand local authorities has developed a concept to identify vulnerable applicants in reception centres and\nin psycho-social centres. The concept was piloted in two reception centres in North Rhine Westphalia and\nBremen and the findings were published in March 2023 in a toolbox of guidelines which provide guidance\nfor counselling and the identification of vulnerabilities.541 The working group continues its work, and the\nFederal government affirms that it supports those Federal States who are interested in implementing the\n\n\n\n\n533\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC.\n534\n      KOK, Betroffene von Menschenhandel im Asylkontext erkennen. However, the guidelines are not legally binding, and the government does not systematically\nmonitor the implementation of the guidelines in the Federal states.542\n\nIn Berlin, a ‘Network for persons with special protection needs’ has developed concepts for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons and their needs since 2008. The network, which refers to itself as a\nunique project in Germany, consists of seven NGOs which cooperate with the social services of the\nregional government. The NGOs have special expertise in the support of the following groups: traumatised\npersons and victims of torture; LGBTQI+; single women and pregnant women; children and\nunaccompanied children; persons with disabilities, with chronic diseases and older persons.543 The\nnetwork was involved in the development of guidelines for the social services to assist with the\nidentification of vulnerable groups.544 The guidelines, published in August 2018, provide detailed\ninformation on how vulnerable persons can be identified and on the determination of special support\nneeds. Social services at the arrival centre Berlin are instructed to systematically screen applicants for\nvulnerability in the reception procedure. If they find that an asylum seeker has special reception needs or\nrequires special procedural guarantees, they try to take appropriate measures (including appointments\nwith specialised institutions) and inform the BAMF and the State authority accordingly.545 In spite of these\nefforts, participating NGOs of the Berlin network have reported that measures to accelerate asylum\nprocedures in the ‘arrival centre’ have had a negative impact on the identification process, since the\ninterview in the asylum procedure is often scheduled before the persons concerned have a chance to\nspeak to staff members of NGOs or of the Federal State institutions.546 In practice when an asylum seeker\nneeds special procedural guarantees, the BAMF assigns ‘special officers’ for the interview (see Special\nprocedural guarantees).547 These officers are trained and experienced decision-makers on various groups\nof vulnerable people (e.g. unaccompanied minors, victims of human trafficking, traumatized persons,\nvictims of torture, gender-specific persecution. These officers shall guarantee that the necessary\nprocedural safeguards are adhered to. NGOs have criticised the fact that special procedural needs of\nasylum seekers are not considered (i.e., the lack of support and time to prepare for an interview).548 In\naddition, identification of a vulnerability by the social services does not entail a right to specific reception\nconditions, which can still be hard to obtain especially since social services and State authorities do not\nalways work hand in hand.549\n\nEspecially since the war in Ukraine has led to rising numbers of protection seekers, systematic\nshortcomings in the identification of vulnerabilities in arrival centres have been documented.550 Despite\nthe pledge for the establishment of a Pre-screening procedure for the identification of vulnerabilities by\n\n\n542\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC; Bundesinitiative Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in\n      Flüchtlingsunterkünften (Federal Initiative for the protection of asylum seekers/refugees in accommodation\n      centres’), BESAFE - Besondere Schutzbedarfe bei der Aufnahme von Geflüchteten erkennen, December\n      2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Kf1jIZ.\n543\n      A list of the project partners of the „Berliner Netzwerk für besonders schutzbedürftige Flüchtlinge’ can be found\n      at: https://bit.ly/3dR5CGU.\n544\n      Leitfaden zur Identifizierung von besonders schutzbedürftigen Geflüchteten in Berlin. However, the guidelines are not legally binding, and the government does not systematically\nmonitor the implementation of the guidelines in the Federal states.542\n\nIn Berlin, a ‘Network for persons with special protection needs’ has developed concepts for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons and their needs since 2008. The network, which refers to itself as a\nunique project in Germany, consists of seven NGOs which cooperate with the social services of the\nregional government. The NGOs have special expertise in the support of the following groups: traumatised\npersons and victims of torture; LGBTQI+; single women and pregnant women; children and\nunaccompanied children; persons with disabilities, with chronic diseases and older persons.543 The\nnetwork was involved in the development of guidelines for the social services to assist with the\nidentification of vulnerable groups.544 The guidelines, published in August 2018, provide detailed\ninformation on how vulnerable persons can be identified and on the determination of special support\nneeds. Social services at the arrival centre Berlin are instructed to systematically screen applicants for\nvulnerability in the reception procedure. If they find that an asylum seeker has special reception needs or\nrequires special procedural guarantees, they try to take appropriate measures (including appointments\nwith specialised institutions) and inform the BAMF and the State authority accordingly.545 In spite of these\nefforts, participating NGOs of the Berlin network have reported that measures to accelerate asylum\nprocedures in the ‘arrival centre’ have had a negative impact on the identification process, since the\ninterview in the asylum procedure is often scheduled before the persons concerned have a chance to\nspeak to staff members of NGOs or of the Federal State institutions.546 In practice when an asylum seeker\nneeds special procedural guarantees, the BAMF assigns ‘special officers’ for the interview (see Special\nprocedural guarantees).547 These officers are trained and experienced decision-makers on various groups\nof vulnerable people (e.g. unaccompanied minors, victims of human trafficking, traumatized persons,\nvictims of torture, gender-specific persecution. These officers shall guarantee that the necessary\nprocedural safeguards are adhered to. NGOs have criticised the fact that special procedural needs of\nasylum seekers are not considered (i.e., the lack of support and time to prepare for an interview).548 In\naddition, identification of a vulnerability by the social services does not entail a right to specific reception\nconditions, which can still be hard to obtain especially since social services and State authorities do not\nalways work hand in hand.549\n\nEspecially since the war in Ukraine has led to rising numbers of protection seekers, systematic\nshortcomings in the identification of vulnerabilities in arrival centres have been documented.550 Despite\nthe pledge for the establishment of a Pre-screening procedure for the identification of vulnerabilities by\n\n\n542\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC; Bundesinitiative Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in\n      Flüchtlingsunterkünften (Federal Initiative for the protection of asylum seekers/refugees in accommodation\n      centres’), BESAFE - Besondere Schutzbedarfe bei der Aufnahme von Geflüchteten erkennen, December\n      2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Kf1jIZ.\n543\n      A list of the project partners of the „Berliner Netzwerk für besonders schutzbedürftige Flüchtlinge’ can be found\n      at: https://bit.ly/3dR5CGU.\n544\n      Leitfaden zur Identifizierung von besonders schutzbedürftigen Geflüchteten in Berlin. If the questionnaire indicates a potential vulnerability,\na screening interview takes place with the socio-psychological service of the Brandenburg immigration\nauthority (Zentrale Ausländerbehörde). Following the screening interview, if a vulnerability is detected\napplicants are referred to psychiatric counselling (which only takes place in Eisenhüttenstadt) and can be\nhoused in a special house for vulnerable applicants. The vulnerability is also communicated to the BAMF.\nHowever, in 2016 this was the case for under 1% of all asylum seekers, indicating that detection rates are\nvery low compared to the estimated prevalence of psychological distress among asylum seekers.\nFurthermore, the special accommodation houses both single men with psychological difficulties and single\nwomen who might have been victims of sexual violence.553 The Brandenburg Refugee Council criticises\nthat while there is a coordinated approach to identify vulnerabilities, the support measures vary depending\non the local governance. Accordingly, in some regions no specialised services for the accommodation of\nasylum seekers with special needs are available.554\n\nIn Lower Saxony, projects to improve the identification of vulnerable groups have been established in\nreception centres first in Friedland in 2012 and have since then expanded to all reception centres in the\nFederal State.555 Upon registration, all applicants are informed about special vulnerabilities during a\nmeeting with the social service of the reception centre and a further diagnosis is carried out in cases\nwhere there are indications of psychological disorders. While the authorities usually follow the\nrecommendations which follow from the diagnosis, between 2015 and 2017 only very few people were\nreferred for a diagnosis.556\n\nIn e.g., Rhineland-Palatinate,557 the regional government has adopted a protection concept which also\nincludes methods for the identification of vulnerabilities.558 This includes the following measures:\n     v Obligation to check for possible vulnerabilities in the reception centres during the initial stages of\n         the reception process and the asylum procedure;\n     v Intensification of communication between various actors and authorities involved in the reception\n         system and in the first steps of the asylum procedure;\n     v Documentation of possible vulnerabilities in a data system used by all authorities involved in the\n         reception process and in the asylum procedure;\n     v Training measures for persons employed by the Federal State in the reception centres to raise\n         awareness on the different forms of vulnerabilities.\n\n551\n      Berlin Senate, Senat beschließt Verfahren zur Unterstützung für besonders schutzbedürftige Geflüchtete aus\n      der Ukraine, 5 April 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48NlJCt.\n552\n      Refugee Council Berlin, Kein Ort für Schutzsuchende: Notunterkunft im Flughafen Tegel schließen, 13 March\n      2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Ufa7Um.\n553\n      See BafF, ‘Identifizierung besonderer Schutzbedürftigkeit am Beispiel von Personen mit\n      Traumafolgestörungen. For\n1,608 of those this measure was ended due to an age assessment.575\n\n      2. Special procedural guarantees\n\n                                    Indicators: Special Procedural Guarantees\n\n      1. Are there special procedural arrangements/guarantees for vulnerable people?\n                                                           Yes        For certain categories      No\n             v If for certain categories, specify which: Unaccompanied children, traumatised persons\n                                                         Victims of torture or violence\n\n         2.1. Adequate support during the interview\n\nThe BAMF does not have specialised units dealing with vulnerable groups. According to the BAMF, all\ncase workers complete the EUAA training module on ‘Interviewing Vulnerable Persons’.576 If an applicant\nor a Federal State authority submits information to the BAMF that indicates vulnerability (such as medical\nrecords or information about specific physical, mental, intellectual or sensory impairments), such\ninformation is transferred to the case worker in charge who can decide to take measures such as allocate\nmore time for the interview, appoint an interpreter of a specific gender or allow the person to bring a\ntrusted person of their choice to the interview.577\n\nFor specific groups of vulnerable persons, the BAMF employs ‘special officers’ (Sonderbeauftragte)\nresponsible for interviews and decisions on claims by applicants with special needs. Special officers also\nadvise their colleagues in dealing with vulnerable applicants and are contact persons for specialised\ncounselling services and psycho-social centres.578 Staff members who become special officers must\ncomplete a training module for the specialisation they want to achieve. In addition, they follow the EASO\ntraining modules for their specialisation.579 Training covers both the identification and in the treatment of\nvulnerable persons.580 According to the BAMF, continuous training is offered for specific topics in the realm\nof the special officers’ responsibilities.581 The BAMF guidelines stipulate that the following cases shall be\nhandled in a particularly sensitive manner and, if necessary, by specially-trained decision-makers:582\n     v Unaccompanied children;\n     v Victims of gender-specific prosecution;\n     v Victims of human trafficking; and\n     v Victims of torture and traumatised asylum seekers.\n\nAs of June 2023, a total of 1,267 BAMF had one or more roles as special officers. This corresponds to\nroughly a third of full-time equivalent positions allocated to the first instance procedure (see Number of\n\n574\n        Nerea González Méndez de Vigo, Alterseinschätzung – ein Irrgarten ohne Ausweg? The distribution among areas of responsibilities was the\nfollowing: Unaccompanied children (410), victims of gender-specific persecution (312) traumatised\npersons and victims of torture (291), victims of trafficking (254).583\n\nSpecially trained case officers may be included at all times of the asylum procedure, or take over, also\nprior to the interview, if vulnerabilitites are known. For example, if it becomes evident during the interview\nthat an asylum seeker belongs to one of these groups, the officer conducting the interview is obliged to\nconsult a special officer, in addition to notifying the reception centre if necessary and authorised by the\napplicant.584 A note on how the officers are planning to proceed must be added by the special officer to\nthe file, particularly if the special officer takes over the case as a result of their consultation. According to\ninformation provided by the government, there is an obligation in cases of unaccompanied minors for\nspecial officers to take over responsibility for the asylum procedures. In other cases of other vulnerable\ngroups, the specially trained case officer must be consulted and there are two options for further\nprocedures: either the special officer adopts an advisory role or they take over responsibility for the\nprocedure.585 However, the BAMF does not record the number of cases in which special officers are\nconsulted or in which procedures are handed over to special officers.586\n\nLawyers have reported that the introduction of special officers has led to some improvement in the\nhandling of ‘sensitive’ cases. The special officers receive special training in the area of LGBTIQ, they\nconduct interviews upon consultation by other BAMF officers and serve as multipliers for their\ncolleagues.587However, there have also been examples of cases in which indications of trauma and even\nexplicit references to torture did not lead to special officers being consulted.588 It has further been criticised\nthat there are shorcomings in the effective implementation of procedural guarantees for LGBTIQ+\npersons, which increases the risk of false decisions.589 There is no individual right to have a special officer\nhandling a person’s case, except for unaccompanied minors for whom this is mandatory. But if evidence\nsuggests that the person is vulnerable or if the person claims to have certain vulnerabilities, the interviewer\nis required to involve a special officer in the procedure, e.g. as consultant.590 However, the Administrative\nCourt of Berlin ruled that if special vulnerabilities have been detected, the absence of a special officer in\nthe asylum procedure constitutes a violation of procedural rights of vulnerable asylum seekers.591\n\n        2.2. Exemption from special procedures\n\nThe German Asylum Act exempts neither unaccompanied children nor persons with special procedural\nguarantees from the airport procedure, despite an express obligation under the recast APD to provide for\nsuch exemptions under certain conditions.592 It also makes no reference to ‘adequate support’ which\nshould be provided to those requiring special procedural guarantees.593\n\n\n\n\n583\n       The government notes that the figures cannot be added since some officers may have qualified in more than\n       one area; Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/7503, 28 June 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3Sb12cF, 2-4.\n584\n       BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. guardians could be responsible for up to 50 minors and were reported to lack sufficient capacity or expertise.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "3B.9", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.4", "8B.5"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "Directly concerns conditions reported in 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The federal legal framework operated through overloaded municipal and Länder youth-welfare systems during Scholz's chancellorship.", "limitations": ["The source does not establish that Scholz personally caused the local capacity shortages.", "The ‘up to eight months’ and workload figures describe reported cases and administrative conditions, not a national average."], "start_unit": 103, "end_unit": 104, "locator": "page 103 through page 104", "exact_excerpt": "municipality in which they had their first contact with authorities or in which they have were\napprehended.618 In this stage of ‘preliminary taking into care’, the local youth welfare office examines\nwhich youth welfare office is ultimately responsible and whether the minor can be subjected to the federal\ndistribution procedure (for details see Age assessment).619 In 2023 it has been criticised that children\nremain in this preliminary stage for up to eight months due to overburdening of local authorities in urban\nareas. In this preliminary stage children only have access to emergency psychological assistance, legal\nrepresentation and youth care services and are often not enrolled for school.620\n\nAfter the responsible youth welfare office has been determined, the regular taking into care procedure is\ninitiated. This procedure is subject to youth welfare law and analogous to the taking into care of youth in\nsituations where their welfare is in acute danger. It includes the appointment of a legal guardian by the\ncompetent Family Court and the so-called ‘clearing procedure’, which includes an examination of whether\nthere are alternatives to an asylum application, such as family reunification in a third country or the\napplication for a residence permit on humanitarian grounds.621\n\nThe guardian represents the minor in all legal matters and is the first contact point for all ‘proceedings\npertaining to asylum and residence law’, including the asylum procedure.622 The legal guardian has to file\nthe asylum application for the unaccompanied minor in written form to the responsible BAMF branch\noffice.623 The guardian acts as the minor’s legal representative, but also as a personal contact person with\nwhom unaccompanied minors can develop perspectives for the future and contribute to the assistance\nplanning procedure carried out by the youth welfare office.624 While the personal interview is conducted\nwith the minor themselves, the legal guardian is present during the interview and may ask them additional\nquestions (i.e. in case the minor forgot to mention an important aspect). They may also request to file\nstatements or explanations on behalf of the minor.625\n\nIn the majority of cases, the youth welfare office acts as guardian for the minor. Often, guardians appointed\nby the youth welfare offices are not in a position to sufficiently support the children in the asylum\nprocedure, because of overburdening, as some guardians in youth welfare offices are responsible for up\nto 50 minors at the same time.626 In 2023 it has been noted that the maximum number of 50 is not kept\n\n\n\n618\n      Gesetz zur Verbesserung der Unterbringung, Versorgung und Betreuung ausländischer Kinder und\n      Jugendlicher, Official Gazette I of 28 October 2015, 1802. Subsequent applications\n\n                                 Indicators: Subsequent Applications\n      1. Does the law provide for a specific procedure for subsequent applications?                   Yes      No\n\n      2. Is a removal order suspended during the examination of a first subsequent application?\n             v At first instance:          Yes             No\n             v At the appeal stage:        Yes             No\n\n      3. Is a removal order suspended during the examination of a second, third, subsequent application?\n              v At first instance:         Yes             No\n              v At the appeal stage:       Yes             No\n\nAs of 2024, some of the information in this section will be affected by the provisions of the new Act on the\nImprovement of Removals of 18 January 2024.\n\nThe law defines a subsequent application (Folgeantrag) as any claim which is submitted after a previous\napplication has been withdrawn or has been finally rejected.632 In case of a subsequent application the\nBAMF conducts a preliminary examination on the admissibility of the application. The admissibility test is\ndetermined by the requirements for resumption of procedures as listed in the Administrative Procedure\nAct.633 According to this, a new asylum procedure is only initiated if one of the following applies:\n    v The material or legal situation on which the decision was based has subsequently changed in\n         favour of the applicant;\n    v New evidence is produced which would have resulted in a more favourable decision for the\n         applicant in the earlier procedure; or\n\n\n\n\n627\n        Federal Association for Unaccompanied Refugee Minors (BumF), Es ist 5 nach 12: Rechtsverletzungen bei\n        unbegleiteten geflüchteten Kindern und Jugendlichen, 21 November 2023, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/3UiVX4x.\n628\n        Uta Rieger und Nerea González Méndez de Vigo, Kindgerechte Ausgestaltung des Asylverfahrens – Eine\n        Bestandsaufnahme, in: Deutsches Kinderhilfswerk e.V. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "From January 2023, Germany replaced state-run individual asylum counselling with federally financed independent counselling by welfare or civil-society organizations and budgeted €20 million for 2023; funding was disbursed only in summer, associations said the amount was insufficient, and staffing could be as low as two full-time counsellors for 600 applicants at one centre.", "question_ids": ["3B.4", "3B.7", "4B.3", "6B.2", "6B.4", "8B.2", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly concerns the 2023 reform, budget, and implementation.", "ruler_attribution": "The federal coalition government enacted the reform and provided the funding; BAMF administered the funding process, while civil-society groups delivered counselling.", "limitations": ["The source does not provide nationwide 2023 uptake figures.", "The staffing example is from one centre and cannot be generalized without further evidence."], "start_unit": 113, "end_unit": 116, "locator": "page 113 through page 116", "exact_excerpt": "Important restrictions to the application of the provision were removed in 2016. In particular, the former\nprovision could only be applied if return to the safe ‘other third country’ was possible within 3 months.\nAlthough this qualification has been removed, the provision has been applied rarely, only 24 times in 2020,\n4 times in 2021, 6 times in 2022 and 3 times in the first half of 2023 (see Admissibility procedure).692\n\n\n G. Information for asylum seekers and access to NGOs and UNHCR\n\n      1. Provision of information on the procedure\n\n                                 Indicators: Information on the Procedure\n      1. Is sufficient information provided to asylum seekers on the procedures, their rights and obligations\n         in practice?                  Yes             With difficulty         No\n\n              v Is tailored information provided to unaccompanied children?                    Yes      No\n\nAccording to Section 24(1) of the Asylum Act, the BAMF:\n\n          ‘... [S]hall inform the foreigner early on in a language he can reasonably be supposed to\n          understand about the course of the procedure and about his rights and duties, especially\n          concerning deadlines and the consequences of missing a deadline, and about possibilities to\n          return voluntarily.’\n\nThe provision was changed with the entry into force of the 2022 Act on the acceleration of asylum court\nproceedings and asylum procedures on 1 January 2023.693 The reform introduced the requirement of\ninforming applicants “early on” instead of “after the lodging of the asylum application”, which was the\nprevious wording. Information is to be provided orally in groups (see\n\nOral information). Another change introduced by the reform is the duty to inform not only about the\nasylum procedure, but also about possibilities to return voluntarily after the rejection of the asylum\napplication.\n\nFor the impact of the Covid-19 outbreak on information provision to asylum applicants see the 2021\nUpdate to the AIDA Country Report for Germany.694\n\n          1.1. Written information\n\nVarious other sections of the Asylum Act also contain obligations on the authorities to inform asylum\nseekers on certain aspects of the procedure. Accordingly, asylum seekers receive various information\nsheets when reporting to the authorities and/or upon arrival at the initial reception centre,695 including the\nfollowing:\n     v An information sheet on the rights and duties during the procedure and on the proceedings in\n        general (‘Belehrung nach § 10 AsylG und allgemeine Verfahrenshinweise’), to be handed out by\n        the authority where an applicant first voices the wish to apply for asylum (the border police, the\n\n\n\n\n692\n        Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/432, 14 January 2022, available in\n        German at: https://bit.ly/3RvW8GL, 6; 19/18498, 2 April 2020, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/3RPHZFG, 6. 20/8222, 5 September 2023, available at: https://bit.ly/3SklJCR. These instructions are available in 44 languages.\nThe applicant has to sign an acknowledgment of the receipt of the information leaflets. In some reception\ncentres, further information is handed out or made available through notice boards or posters (e.g.\ninformation on the office hours of authorities, NGOs and other institutions), but there is no systematic\npractice for the distribution of such additional information.\n\nIt has been a long-standing criticism from lawyers and NGOs that both the written instructions and the\noral briefings provided by the Federal Office are ‘rather abstract and standardised’.706 In 2016 it was\nparticularly criticised, that the information is notsuitable to render the significance and content of questions\nduring interviews sufficiently understandable to applicants. Oral information\n\nOral information for asylum applicants now mainly consists of the ‘voluntary independent state-run\ncounselling’ that was introduced with the so-called ‘Orderly-Return-Law’, in force since 21 August 2019\n(Section 12a Asylum Act). With the entry into force of the 2022 Act on the acceleration of asylum court\nproceedings and asylum procedures710 on 1 January 2023, the state-run counselling is to be replaced by\nindependent counselling, financed by the Federal Government but carried out by welfare associations or\n‘other civil society actors’.711 This is in line with long-standing demands form welfare associations (see\nbelow). Counselling consists of two stages: group sessions with basic information on the asylum\nprocedure as well as on return procedures, followed by the second stage of individual counselling\nsessions. The BAMF will continue to carry out the first stage of counselling as described below, whereas\nindependent organisations will carry out individual counselling.712 The funding process for independent\ncounselling associations started in February 2023 where associations could file interest for funding. After\na summary oversight, the BAMF then required the associations to file the encompassing application for\nfunding.713 EUR 20 million of financing were provided for in 2023. Welfare organisations criticise that the\nmoney was only disbursed in the summer of 2023, which delayed the availability of independent\ncounselling or caused financial gaps for those associations which provided counselling services prior to\nthe official distribution of funding. Additionally, NGOs have been criticial of the amount foreseen, stating\nthat EUR 20 million is not sufficient for nationwide independent counselling. From the AnkER centre in\nManching-Ingoldstadt, the NGO in charge can currently offer two fulltime counselling positions for up to\n600 asylum applicants, even though the BAMF’s general recommendation is one fulltime position for 180\nasylum applicants.714 The sum to be spent for personnel suffices only for early career and not for\nexperienced personnel, which make it difficult to find employees and which in combination with the high\nnumber of cases, causes an overburdening of the staff.715 Despite the envisaged goal of EUR 80 million\nannually, for 2024 again only EUR 25 million are calculated, this time for the whole year, not as in 2023\nfor the second half of the year.716 According to welfare associations, the insecurity as to how much funding\nwill be provided in the upcoming years and under which circumstances the funding will be awarded has\nled to the withdrawal of associations from their funding applications for the counselling service.717\n\n\n\n\n707\n      Memorandum Alliance, Memorandum für faire und sorgfältige Asylverfahren in Deutschland. Since there are no federal rules governing the access, it is up to the discretion of the local\nauthorities whether welfare associations have access to the centres. In Munich, the Refugee Council\ntried to provide independent mobile counselling prior 2023 and has been denied access. The Federal\nAdministrative Court upheld the denial in 2023. The court decided that access must be granted in\nindividual case after registration and only where an asylum applicant has demanded counselling.\nHowever, local authorities are not obliged to grant open access to the facilities.718 This leads to legal\nuncertainty as to whether systematic access will be provided to welfare associations under the new rules\non counselling. Overall, several associations criticise that due to the lack of funding, the uncoordinated\nfunding process and the legal uncertainty as to whether access to accommodations centres is provided,\naccess to individual counselling cannot be guaranteed in Germany.\n\nPrior to the reforms in January 2023, government advice covered the period from the lodging of the asylum\napplication to the explanation of a first instance decision; now the legal conselling can also cover appeal\nproceedings.719 According to the BAMF, the staff who offered the counselling underwent a one-week\ntraining and was ‘organisationally separated from the asylum area’.720 Procedure counselling was first\nintroduced in a pilot project together with welfare associations.721 It was then established first in all AnkER\nand functionally equivalent centres and has been rolled out to the rest of the BAMF branch offices since\n2020.722\n\nAs of 31 December 2022, counselling was available in 46 BAMF branch offices.723 Throughout 2022,\n37,644 applicants took part in the first stage counselling, while 3,147 received individual counselling\n(second stage). This is an increase in comparison to 2021 (1,928 individual sessions, while 25,784\npersons took part in group sessions), but still shows that only around 15% of the 244,132 persons who\napplied for asylum in 2022 (see Statistics) received individual advice. More information on counselling\nduring the Covid-19 outbreak can be found in the 2021 Update to the AIDA Country Report for Germany.724\nNo information on the availability of counselling and on the number of sessions is available for 2023 as of\nApril 2024.\n\nThe BAMF counselling sessions represent an improvement compared to the situation prior to August 2019\nwhen no information was systematically provided to asylum seekers.725 Nevertheless, the system is\nheavily criticised by NGOs as group counselling sessions tend to be organised within a very short period\nbefore the personal interview with the BAMF and the information provided is limited (i.e. the BAMF tends\nto provide general information on the asylum procedure, sometimes focusing only on asylum seekers’\nobligations and also on information which has nothing to do with the procedure, such as the so-called\n‘return options’).726\n\nIn addition to the counselling services as regulated by the asylum act, asylum seekers are orally informed\nabout ‘the significance and the proceedings of the interview’ and they are instructed about their rights and\n\n\n718\n      Federal Administrative Court, Decision 1 C 40.21, 28 March 2023, available in Germant at:\n      https://bit.ly/480lN0o, para. 27f.\n719\n      Section 12a (2) German Asylum Act.\n720\n      See AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2022, April 2023, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UChWUr, 99.\n721\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "Germany's federal Afghan humanitarian admission programme selected 229 people by 30 June 2023, but only 13 had entered Germany by October; the programme was paused in March over alleged misuse, the government said there was no evidence of systemic abuse, and it restarted on 26 June after additional security screening was introduced.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "3B.9", "6B.3", "8B.6", "8B.8", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly concerns programme decisions and implementation during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The federal government, Interior Ministry, Foreign Office and security agencies operated the programme under Scholz's chancellorship.", "limitations": ["The source reports allegations and the government's rebuttal but does not independently determine whether misuse occurred.", "The admission numbers reflect implementation through October 2023, not the entire programme period."], "start_unit": 121, "end_unit": 124, "locator": "page 121 through page 124", "exact_excerpt": "subsequent applications compared to 15,259 in 2021 (see also Error! Reference source not\nfound.Subsequent applications). The number of ‘upgrade appeal’ cases and decisions remains high,\nhowever, likely as a result of long court procedures. Between January and the end of May 2023, courts\ndecided on 5.736 such appeals, and in 806 cases (14%) granted asylum or refugee protection, while in\n4,930 cases (86%) the appeal did not lead to an improvement in the protection status.753 9,525 such\nappeals of Syrian nationals were pending as of 31 May 2023, a similarly high number to the end of 2022\n(9,458).754\n\nThe removal ban for Syria that had been in place since 2012 expired at the end of December 2020. The\nban was based on a common decision of the Federal States and the Federal government, but could not\nbe renewed due to disagreement regarding the possibility to remove criminals and ‘persons posing a risk’\nrelated to terrorist activities (‘Gefährder’).This was heavily criticised by NGOs and organisations such as\nthe German Institute for Human Rights, UNHCR and Caritas.755 The removal statistics for the first half of\n2023 indicate that 410 removals of Syrian nationals took place.756 However, Syria is not listed as a country\nof destination for removals in the first half of 2023, meaning that the removals of Syrian nationals took\nplace to other countries, for example to other EU Member States in the form of Dublin transfers or\nremovals following a refusal of entry.757 As of February 2023, the Federal Government declared that it\ncurrently sees no possibilities for removals to Syria.758\n\n      2. Afghanistan\n\nEmergency evacuation since the Taliban takeover in 2021\n\nWith the takeover of the Taliban on 15 August 2021, the German government started an evacuation\noperation for German nationals in Afghanistan as well as Afghan nationals who had worked for German\nauthorities, the military and ‘especially endangered persons’. Between 16 and 26 August 2021, a total of\n5,300 persons were evacuated, out of which 4,400 Afghan nationals. The evacuated persons entered\nGermany via an emergency visa (based on Section 14 and 22 Residence Act).759 Upon arrival, the BAMF\nthen examined whether persons had already been granted permission for an admission from abroad\n(Section 22 Residence Act). If this was not the case, and if the Federal Ministry decided no such\npermission could be granted, persons were informed of this and of the possibility to apply for asylum in\nGermany. 760\n\nAfter the end of the evacuation, German authorities continued to receive and examine notifications of risk\nof former employees and of “especially endangered individuals” in exceptional circumstances. Sub-\ncontractors and consultants who worked for German authorities only indirectly are considered on a case\nby case basis according to the Federal Government. 761 If the examination confirms that the persons are\nat risk due to their work for a German authority, admission permissions continue to be granted according\n\n753\n       Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/5709, 17 February 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3K3w3MX, 43.\n754\n       Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/8222, 5 September 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3SklJCR and 20/5709, 17 February 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/3K3w3MX, 41.\n755\n       FRA (European Union Fundamental Rights     BAMF, Migrationsbericht 2020 der Bundesregierung, December 2021, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/3nTDv1J, 38.\n761\n       Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The and Community.762 Permissions are also included for close family\nmembers (spouses and minor siblings), other relatives are only considered in hardship cases.763 Since\ntravelling out of Afghanistan is difficult and costly, the Federal Government has provided 32 million Euros\nin funding to the GiZ (the German Development Agency) to support persons in leaving the country. It has\nbeen criticised that despite the acknowledgement that fleeing Afghanistan is difficult, there is no\nsystematic evacuation scheme.764 As a result, there have been reports that persons who managed to\ndepart from Afghanistan have been pushed back by Bulgarian, Turkish and Greek border police.765\nPersons admitted to Germany mainly leave via Pakistan and Iran. 766 Germany has also concluded an\nagreement with the government of Pakistan to allow the concerned persons to enter Pakistan with a\nTazkira K (ID card) instead of a passport as required by the Taliban to leave the country. However, in mid-\nSeptember 2022 the Federal Government reported that a total of 34 former employees and family\nmembers are known to have died in Afghanistan.767\n\nAdmission schemes\n\nGermany has been operating an admission scheme for local staff of German ministries in Afghanistan\nsince 2013. The scheme is based on Art. 22 (2) Residence Act (Temporary residence permission to uphold\nthe political interest of the Federal Republic of Germany). The eligibility criteria depend on the status of\nthe former employee. Only former staff (and their close family members) directly employed by German\nentities are covered by the programme.768\n\nFrom the takeover of the Taliban in 2021 to 17 October 2022, according to the Federal Government,\n38,100 persons had been issued a permission for admission to Germany (out of which 24,500 were former\nemployees and eligible family members, and 13,600 were especially vulnerable persons and their eligible\nfamily members). Around 26,000 of these (68.2%) persons had entered Germany up until that time. As of\n10 December 2021, a total of 28,053 permissions for admission from abroad had been issued to Afghan\nnationals and 8,014 persons had entered Germany as of the same date.769 The admission scheme for\nlocal staff continues in parallel to the new humanitarian admission scheme announced on 17 October\n2022.\n\nOn 17 October 2022, the Federal Government launched an additional federal admissions programme\nwhich had been announced in the coalition agreement of 2021.770 The government describes the\nprogramme and procedure as follows: the programme is geared towards persons who ‘have exposed\nthemselves to particular risk through their commitment to women’s and human rights or their work in the\nspheres of justice, politics, the media, education, culture, sport or academia and are thus vulnerable’ or\n‘due to the special circumstances of their individual cases have experienced or are experiencing violence\nor persecution based on their gender, sexual orientation or gender identity or religion and are therefore at\nconcrete and personal risk. In particular, these are victims of serious individual women’s rights violations,\nhomo- or transphobic human rights violations or vulnerable representatives of religious\n\n\n\n\n762\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community, Reply to written question by Canan Bayram (The Greens),\n      20/5046, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3NvUljF, 81-121.\n763\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 11.\n764\n      Taz.de, Die Mauern werden höher, 27 February 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48d59Ln.\n765\n      Ibid.\n766\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 2.\n767\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 16.\n768\n      ECRE, Afghans Seeking Protection in Europe, December 2021, available at: https://bit.ly/3krGfED, 11.\n769\n      Deutscher Bundestag, parliamentary question by The Left, 20/791, 22 February 2022, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3RPrRUC, 1.\n770\n      AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2021, April 2022, available at: https://bit.ly/3XnN7RS, 11.\n\n\n                                                      122\n\ngroups/communities.’771 The admission programme includes family members of those persons, which\nincludes spouses or same sex partners, minor children and other family members who can prove a\nrelation of dependency (beyond economic dependency) with the main person and find themselves in a\nsituation of concrete and lasting danger due to the work or vulnerability of the main person.772 The German\ngovernment appoints agencies (including civil society organisations) who can put forward names of\nsuitable persons, who must still be living in Afghanistan, via an IT application containing a questionnaire\nof a total of 41 pages.773 The names of these organisations are not made public by the government, but\naccording to a press report, PRO ASYL, Reporters without Borders, Mission Lifeline and Luftbrücke Kabul\nare taking part in the programme as of 20 December 2022.774\n\nThe Government then takes the admission decision based on selection criteria that include vulnerability\n(in line with the UNHCR catalogue of criteria), relation to Germany e. g. through language skills, family\nties, previous stays or work for German authorities or projects, level of personal exposure of the person\ne. g. through a visible / exposed position or public statements, and a special political interest on the side\nof Germany to admit a person.775 As with the previous admission programme, selected persons first\nreceive assistance to leave Afghanistan and enter a neighbouring country and are then issued a visa and\ntravel assistance by the German embassy in that country. Persons who enter Germany under the\nprogramme receive a residence permit for three years. The Federal State responsible for reception of the\npersons is to be determined according to the quota system for the distribution of asylum seekers (see\nRegistration of the asylum application), although family ties and other ‘criteria supporting integration’ are\nto be taken into account.776\n\nWhen announcing the programme, the Federal Government declared that ‘the new programme is now to\nbe implemented quickly’ and that it planned to approve around 1,000 requests per month, which is about\nthe amount of permissions granted in the months preceding the announcement. The programme is\nplanned to run until the end of the current government’s term in 2025.777 As of 30 June 2023, 229 persons\nhave been selected for admission.778 As of October 2023, only 13 of them had been admitted, due to the\npause of the admission procedure (see below).779 According to a press report, the NGOs Mission Lifeline\nand Luftbrücke Kabul alone have received around 32,000 requests as of early November 2022.780\n\nIn March 2023, the Federal Foreign office declared that all admission programs would be put to a halt for\nan indefinite time due to alleged abuse. The German newspaper Cicer and Bild published that, according\nto information they received, the admission programs had been used to bring radical Islamists to\n\n\n\n771\n      Federal Foreign Office, Joint press release by the Federal Foreign Office and the Federal Ministry of the\n      Interior and Community and Community on the federal admission programme for people from Afghanistan\n      who are at particular risk, 17 October 2022, available at: http://bit.ly/3J47ZJA.\n772\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n773\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n774\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n775\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n776\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, Anordnung des\n      Bundesministeriums des Innern und für Heimat gemäß § 23 Absatz 2, Absatz 3 i. A speaker of the\nForeigner’s Office declared that there were no evidence supporting a systemic misuse of the admission\nprograms.782 Only in one case a person who applied for admission has been identified as a possible\n‘threat’ (‘Gefährder’). Nevertheless, the Foreigners Office along with the Minister of Interior decided to\nintroduce additional screening mechanisms and to halt the admission program until the screening\nmechanism is in place.783 The screening procedure involves an automatic data reconciliation with\ndatabases from the national security agencies and interviews with the German domestic intelligence\nservice (Bundesamt für Verfassungsschutz), Federal Criminal Police Office (Bundeskriminalamt), the\nFederal Police or BAMF officers acting on behalf of these national security services.784 The admission\nprograms were restarted from 26 June 2023. In the first months of the newly introduced screening\nprocedure (26 June – 21 July 2023) 99 screening interviews took place, no security concerns have been\nidentified among those.785 The Federal government further states, that the capacities for the screening\nmechanism have been expanded since its start. Accordingly, as of August 2023 several hundreds of\nscreenings can be conducted per month.786\n\nThe Left party and NGOs such as PRO ASYL welcomed the launch of the programme but criticised that\n1,000 admissions per month was too low given the ‘real pressure of persecution’ for ‘people who have\nfought for democracy and human rights’. PRO ASYL further criticised that the relatively abstract selection\ncriteria could lead to an ‘ethically highly ambivalent protection lottery’787 especially in connection with the\nfact that only authorised agencies could put forward people and that the application is through an\nalgorithm-based IT application with little room to put forward specific individual circumstances.788 The\nNGO Kabul Luftbrücke reported problems with the IT application in October and November, leading to\ndelays in sending the online forms to authorities. A further point of critique is that the programme does not\nextend to persons who have managed to flee Afghanistan.789 Several NGOs also voiced concerns over\nthe practical implementation, demanding a better staffing of the counselling and coordination centres for\nthe programme and questioning the ‘organization and content’ of the procedure,790 especially given that\nit is required to have a passport in order to leave the country while obtaining one is made extremely\ndifficult by the Taliban government. One year after the official launch, in October 2023, several NGOs\nreflected on the development of the program. They demanded that the originally envisaged number of\n1,000 admissions per months should be fulfilled, that the procedure should be more transparent and that\nin additional admission schemes are necessary to meet the needs for protection.791\n\nIn addition to the Federal Government, several Federal States (Thuringia, Berlin, Hessen and Bremen)\nhave implemented admission programmes based on family ties to Afghans living in the respective Federal\nStates (for more information see Family Reunification). However, the programs in Thuringia and Bremen\n\n\n\n781\n      Cicero, Bundesregierung holt Scharia-Richter nach Deutschland, 3 March 2023, available in German at:\n "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "In 2023 reception capacity was severely strained: Berlin and Hamburg were around 97% occupied, Berlin required 8,000 additional places, and overcrowded facilities produced reports of cold tents, illness, inadequate sanitation, lack of privacy, registration backlogs, insufficient health care, and violent conflicts; the federal government and states agreed on additional federal aid in November 2023.", "question_ids": ["3B.6", "3B.7", "3B.9", "5B.6", "5B.9", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.6", "8B.5", attributes the funding agreement to the federal government and federal states, while accommodation operations are primarily local and state responsibilities.", "limitations": ["The cited harms are "regulations provide for 6 or 7m² per person.971 A typical room in an initial reception centre has between 2\nand 4 beds, there are chairs and a table and each resident has a locker for themselves. Size of rooms\nmay vary, but rooms with a single bed are highly exceptional.\n\nMost initial reception centres have a policy to accommodate single women and families in separate\nbuildings or separate wings of their buildings. The AnKER centre in Manching/Ingolstadt for example\nprovides separate rooms for vulnerable persons.\n\nBath and toilet facilities usually consist of shower rooms and toilets which people have to share. As\nmentioned before, there are no regulations that oblige the Federal states to fulfil any standards. However,\nthe Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women and Youth and UNICEF have worked out\na policy paper stating minimum standards concerning the protection of refugees in reception centres.972\nThese guidelines state that sanitary facilities should be separate between genders and that those facilities\nshould be built in a way that guarantees privacy. Other guidelines recommend that one shower should be\navailable for 10 to 12 persons, but in some reception centres the ratio is worse than that, particularly in\nsituations of overcrowding. Cleaning of shared space (halls, corridors) as well as of sanitary facilities is\ncarried out by external companies in the initial reception centres.\n\nFood is supplied in the initial reception centres and is usually served in canteens on the premises of the\ncentres. In general, two or more menus are on offer for lunch and the management of the catering facilities\ntries to ensure that specific food is provided with regard to religious sentiments. Some, but not all initial\nreception centres also have shared kitchen space which enables asylum seekers to cook their own food;\nin AnkER centres, for instance, cooking is not allowed. Refrigerators for the use of asylum seekers are\navailable in some initial reception centres, but this seems to be the exception. In some centres, the\nmanagement does not allow hot water boilers for asylum seekers as this would be forbidden by fire\nregulations.973\n\nThe living conditions in many initial reception centres have been criticised by asylum seekers, volunteers\nand NGOs – especially in light of the extended obligatory stay in these facilities. In 2022 the conditions\ndeteriorated even more due to the massive overcrowding as consequence from the war in Ukraine and\nthe situation in Afghanistan. But still in 2023, small cities like Pulheim in North Rhine-Westphalia,974 as\nwell as middle size municipalities like Augsburg in Bavaria975 and Aachen in North Rhine-Westphalia976\nbut also big municipalities like Berlin977 and Hamburg978 face difficulties in accommodating new\nprotection seekers. In Berlin and Hamburg, around 97% percent of the reception capacities were\noccupied around October and November 2023.979\n\n\n\n\n971\n      European Migration Network, The Organisation of Reception Facilities for Asylum Seekers in Germany, 2013,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/48ojgO9, 26.\n972\n      Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women and Youth and UNICEF, Mindeststandards\n      zum Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in Flüchtlingsunterkünften, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/48WAaV2.\n973\n      PROASYL and Refugee Council Berlin, Das Asylbewerberleistungsgesetz – Einschränkungen des\n      Grundrechts auf ein menschenwürdiges Existenzminimum für Geflüchtete, November 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3XrdSox, 100.\n974\n       WDR.de, Geflüchtete klagen über menschenunwürdige Zustände in Pulheimer Flüchtlingsunterkünften,\n      available at: https://bit.ly/3SKjme1.\n975\n      Augsburger Allgemeine, Augsburg gerät bei der Unterbringung von Geflüchteten wieder stärker unter Druck,\n      19 August 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/42MZ1Iq.\n976\n      InfoMigrants, ‘Refugees: German cities are reaching their limits’, 26 September 2022, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3wpxjCp.\n977\n      Silke Mehring, Leichtbauhallen in Tegel geplant Unterbringung von Ukraine-Flüchtlingen in Berlin wird immer\n      komplizierter, rbb24, 02 December.2022, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3wdFPUT.\n978\n      Ibid.\n979\n      Rbb24.de, Berlin droht Verlust von tausenden Unterkunftsplätzen für Geflüchtete, 28 Novermber 2023,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/48nVWzN; NDR, Flüchtlingszahlen: Senat sieht Hamburg am Limit, 6\n      October 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3SHfJ7w.\n\n\n                                                     151\n\nAccording to the administration of Berlin, 10,000 additional places were required in 2022.980 At the end\nof September 2023, the senate administration of Berlin spoke again of 8,000 more required places until\nthe end of 2023.981 Those necessary places are to be created in gymnasiums, hotels, exhibitions halls\nand through further usage of the old airport Tegel. In October 2023, the initial reception centre in Suhl,\nThuringia, was completely full and had imposed a freeze on admissions.982 However, the municipalities\ncannot offer any other accommodation facilities as all centres and apartments are full. The only possibility\nleft, according to the Thuringian Association of Towns and Municipalities, is the usage of sport halls.983\nMunicipalities in North Rine-Westphalia have started to rent hotels to provide accommodation for asylum\nseekers.984 Although this practice is very costly, it is preferred over blocking gymnasiums of local sport\nassociations. The authorities on the local, state and federal level blame each other for the shortcomings.\nWhile the local authorities are by law responsible for the accommodation of protection seekers,985 they\nclaim that the do not have enough financial and housing resources to fulfil the current need. They therefore\nask the Federal States to vacate more housing properties.986 The Federal States in turn urge the Federal\ngovernment to strengthen their efforts and to take up a coordinating role.987 After months of conflict, the\nFederal government and the Federal States have agreed in November 2023 – next to some deterrent\nmeasures that should decrease migration – upon more financial aid by the Federal Government.988\n\nAs consequence of the overcrowding, the authorities seem to be overburdened and deteriorating\nconditions have been reported. Under the law the state may derogate from the obligation to stay in initial\nreception centres in cases of overcrowding.989 Nevertheless, so far only Berlin has used this derogation\nclause and allows asylum seekers who have been allocated to Berlin under the “Köngisteiner Schlüssel”\nto live in private accommodations since the end of January 2023.990 In emergency shelters e.g. in Berlin,\nit has been reported that the tents at the former Berlin-Tegel airport do not protect from the cold causing\nnumerous illnesses and facilitating the spread of Covid-19 (on conditions in reception facilities during the\nCovid-19 pandemic see the 2021 Update).991 One partitioned area on such a tent is in general shared by\neight people. As a result, each asylum seeker has, according to calculations by the refugee council Berlin,\nonly about 2,6 m2 available for themselves.992 Additionally, since the airport is surrounded by barbed wire\nno systematic access for NGOs and volunteers is granted. At the same time protection seekers need to\ntake a shuttle bus to enter and exit the emergency shelter, thereby making it difficult for protection seekers\nto access legal aid and social assistance. In the emergency tents in Bremen protection seekers report of\nnon-functional and unclean sanitary facilities, coldness due to non-functional heating systems and a tense\natmosphere.993 Only one month later the municipality of Bremen decided to evacuate the tents due to the\n\n980\n      Thomas Frey, Ukraine-Ankunftszentrum wird um 3200 Plätze erweitert, Berliner Woche 06 January 2023,\n      available in German at: http://bit.ly/3CY9Jjy.\n981\n      Senatskanzlei Berlin, Mehr Plätze für die Unterbringung von Geflüchteten, press release from 26 September\n      2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3wnKSpd.\n982\n      Mdr.de, Erstaufnahmeeinrichtungen für Flüchtlinge – Was tun, wenn sie voll sind?, 6 October 2023, available\n      in German at: https://bit.ly/3I9iV74.\n983\n      Ibid.\n984\n      Wdr.de, Flüchtlinge in Kommunen: Letzter Ausweg Hotel, 26 January 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UPlLFY.\n985\n      §44 AsylG in conjunction with the different Federal state’s Reception laws: e.g. §1 Landesaufnahmegesetz\n      Hesse; Inhabitants were partially relocated to emergency shelters on\nexhibition grounds. This solution was only temporary and local authorities have organised lightweight\nbuilding constructions which should serve until mid-2024.994 Even if asylum seekers do not live in tents\nbut in houses, the living conditions are in many cases catastrophic. In a reception centre in Pulheim,\nNorth Rhine-Westphalia, for example, the building smells heavily, the sanitary facilities are mouldy and\nrats run around the complex.995\n\nIn arrival centres, the overcrowding mostly leads to backlogs in the registration procedure and conflicts\namong the protection seekers stemming from the lack of privacy. Asylum seekers at the arrival centre in\nHamburg-Rahlstedt, for example, have reported inter alia a backlog of registration,996 lack of privacy,\nunclean sanitary facilities and disturbances at night. The sleeping areas are placed in former warehouses\nand divided by thin partitions into several compartments, which do not allow for privacy. Besides reading\nlamps attached to each bed, there is one common light for the whole warehouse, which is switched on\nfrom 8:00am to 22:00pm.997 A backlog of registration, lack of access to health care and social assistance\nhas been reported also from the arrival centre in Berlin.998 In the arrival centre in Thuringia, many violent\nconflicts have been reported stemming from the lack of staff members, stressed social workers and non-\ntrained security personnel.999 The backlog of registrations all over Germany has supposedly been caught\nup until the end of June 2023, according to the Federal Government.1000\n\nMore generally, studies published in 2020 have come to the conclusion that the accommodation in initial\nreception centres infringes upon children’s’ rights and constitutes a danger to their mental health. The\nspatial confinement, the experience of violence and removals, as well as the permanent uncertainty cause\npsychological stress and have a negative impact on children.1001 Health care and psychosocial support\nprovided for young refugees in the mass accommodations were described as worryingly inadequate for\nmost of the facilities.1002 The study of PROASYL and the Refugee Council Berlin support these findings.\nAccording to the study of 2022, especially the access to health care, access to adequate hygienic and\nother products such as strollers for toddlers are scarce.1003\n\nThe NGO ‘Ärzte der Welt’ (Doctors of the World) announced in September 2019 that an advice service\nrun by the organisation in the AnkER-centre of Manching/Ingolstadt was to be terminated. The NGO\ndescribed living conditions in the facility as ‘morbid’ and claimed that adequate treatment, in particular\ntreatment of persons with psychological disorders, was impossible under the circumstances. Insufficient\nprotection against assaults, lack of privacy and nocturnal disturbances were impeding the mental\nstabilisation of asylum-seekers at the facility and the NGO was no longer capable to bear responsibility\nfor the mental health of its patients. Moreover, the organisation claims that there was no system for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons in place at the facility.1004\n\n994\n       Weserreport, Geflüchtete ziehen um, 10 January 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3wrtM9V.\n995\n       WDR.de, Geflüchtete klagen über menschenunwürdige Zustände in Pulheimer Flüchtlingsunterkünften,\n       available at: https://bit.ly/3SKjme1.\n996\n       Redaktionsnetzwerk Deutschland (RND), Streit in Hamburg: CDU wirft Senat unhaltbare Zustände in zentraler\n       Ankunftsstelle vor, 8 March 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3XXoR9e.\n997\n       Fluchtpunkt, Mitten in Hamburg, und doch am Rand: Unzumutbare Bedingungen im Ankunftszentrum\n       Rahlstedt, November 14th 2019, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3iJgGtb.\n998\n       Taz.de, Ankunftszentrum überlaufen, 5 January 2023, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3ZZmEMf.\n999\n       MDR, Personalnot und Überforderung: Probleme in Erstaufnahmeeinrichtung nehmen zu, 29 November 2022,\n       available in German at: https://bit.ly/3GZS6B6.\n1000\n       Federal Government, response to information request by The Left, 20/8222, 05 September 2023, available in\n       German at: https://bit.ly/48E1rL3.\n1001\n       Terre des hommes, Zur Lebenssituation von minderjährigen Geflüchteten in Aufnahmeeinrichtungen, June\n       2020, available in German at: https://bit.ly/38Etah0, 7.\n1002\n       Bundesweite Arbeitsgemeinschaft der Psychosozialen Zentren für Flüchtlinge und Folteropfer (BAfF), Living\n       in a box - While there are ‘best practice’ examples\nin some regions for the integration of refugee children into the education system, obstacles remain in\nother places, such as lack of access to language and literacy courses or to regular schools.1048 One such\nbest practice example for education during the Covid-19 pandemic is the district of Treptow-Köpenick in\nBerlin, which deployed mobile teams and tablets to support distance learning of children and youth living\nin youth welfare facilities in 2021.1049 In general, however, it remains to say that German schools are full\nand overwhelmed with the organization of special needs classes.\n\nAccess to education in initial reception centres\n\nAccess to education is particularly problematic in initial reception centres such as arrival and AnkER\ncentres. Especially the lack of sufficient internet access and digital infrastructure in many reception\ncentres, make it difficult to access education offers which have been moved online, even after COVID.1050\nIn 2016, an association of various NGOs (regional refugee councils, Federal Association for\nUnaccompanied Refugee Minors, Youth without Borders) started a campaign called ‘School for all’\n(Schule für alle) to draw attention to the fact that children in many initial reception centres have only had\nvery basic schooling and no access to the regular school system for the duration of their stay in these\nfacilities (see Freedom of Movement: Obligation to Stay in Initial Reception Centres). The Federal Ministry\nfor Education and Research partly acknowledged the shortcomings and launched a programme to\nfacilitate the early access to educational material. Along with the ‘Foundation reading’ (Stiftung Lesen)\nthe Federal Ministry aims to distribute reading material to arrival centres to support children and their\nfamilies in gaining access to the German language.1051 Furthermore, NGOs have criticised the fact that\naccess to education services is severely limited for asylum seekers above the age of 16, many of whom\nhave not finished school in their countries of origin and therefore need access to the school system in\norder to gain a degree.1052\n\nThese problems continue to exist today. In 2021 the Leibniz Institute for Educational Trajectories started\na comprehensive study called ‘ReGES – Refugees in the German Educational System’.1053 The first\npreliminary findings suggest, that especially the regional differences in how and when access to the\nschooling system is granted for children seeking asylum highly impacts the participation opportunities of\nchildren.1054 The team of researchers identified four main factors which influence the educational\nparticipation. Whereas family and individual resources seem to play a minor role, external factors\nstemming from the regulatory system of the different Federal states predominantly determine participation\nin the educational system. Four factors have been identified as influential: First, the duration until school\nenrolment in Germany.1055 Half of all federal states exempt asylum-seeking children from compulsory\n\n1048\n       For an overview of practices regarding the integration of refugee children into schools as of 2018, see See\n       Julian Tangermann and Paula Hoffmeyer-Zlotnik, ‘Unaccompanied Minors in Germany – Challenges and\n       Measures after the Clarification of Residence Status’, March 2018, available in English at\n       https://bit.ly/3KcEEe6, 56-57.\n1049\n       Bezirksamt Treptow-Köpenick, ‘Mobile Unterstützung in der Pandemie: Jugend-Lern-Hilfe für Kinder und\n       Jugendliche in Heimen‘, 05 January 2021, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3J5jvDc.\n1050\n       See AWO Bundesverband, Unterbringung von geflüchteten Menschen und die Corona-Pandemie.\n       Forderungen an die Politik und Empfehlungen an die Praxis, October 2021, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/3fDYj8U, 18.\n1051\n       Federal Ministry for Education and Research, ‚Stark-Watzinger: Flüchtlingskindern Bildungschancen\n       ermöglichen‘, press release 3 January 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Dm032G.\n1052\n       See the campaign at: http://kampagne-schule-fuer-alle.de/.\n1053\n       Leibniz Institute for Educational Trajectories, ‘ReGES – Refugees in the Germene Education System’, project\n       description available at: http://bit.ly/407TcDL.\n1054\n       G. Second, the type of class attended and third,\naccess to different types of schools is important. Here the preliminary data suggests that the more\nrestricted children are in choosing their path in the educational system, the less chances of participating\nin the regular educational system they have. Here more research is required, as to the research team.\nFourth, the flexibility of the school system on age-appropriate placement in school classes impacts the\nparticipation of asylum-seeking children in the educational system.1057\n\nProblems with access to the education system have particularly been reported with regard to initial\nreception centres renamed as AnKER centres in Bavaria in 2018. The general policy foresees the\nprovision inside the AnkER centres of both schooling for children aged 6-16 and professional school\n(Berufschule) for persons aged 16-21. The AnKER centre in Regensburg is one of the only facilities\nallowing children up to the age of 16 to go to regular schools. This was originally only made possible\nbecause the authorities did not manage to build the necessary facilities on time, but has stayed that way.\nHowever, persons aged 16-21 are provided education in containers in the centre, not at school.1058\n\nIn the AnKER centre in Manching/Ingolstadt classes are provided within the facility. The classes mainly\nfocus on German language, but also cover maths and other subjects. A certificate is provided upon\ncompletion of the course. However, asylum seekers do not undergo examinations at the end of the year\nsince people stay for shorter periods. If an asylum seeker wishes to access regular schools, a test\nassessing their capacity to attend classes in regular schools is conducted, namely to assess German\nlanguage level.1059 This was done following successful litigation in March 2018, when Manching/Ingolstadt\nwas a ‘transit centre’, which led authorities to grant access to regular schools for six children from Kosovo,\nafter an Administrative Court had decided that children from these centres with sufficient German\nlanguage skills had the right to attend the regular school system.1060\n\nThe problem of lack of access to the education system in initial reception centres may have been mitigated\nto a certain extent by the legal clarification, introduced in 2019, according to which the general maximum\ntime-limit for a stay in initial reception centres is of six months for families with minor children. Because of\nthis amendment, children should be housed in decentralised accommodation after a few months (possibly\nearlier than the maximum six-months time-limit allows), which should in turn result in them having access\nto regular schools at their new place of residence. By way of example, in Saxony the authorities have ‘an\nestablished policy’ of allocating families with school-age children to municipalities within three months.1061\n\nVocational training and higher education\n\nIn legal terms, asylum seekers generally have access to vocational training. In order to start vocational\ntraining, they need an employment permit.1062 However, the fact that asylum seeker's permission to stay\n(Aufenthaltsgestattung) are issued for a 6-month-period frequently renders access to vocational training\nimpossible. Training contracts usually have to be concluded for a duration of two or three years.
